Mackenzie King</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Joseph-Enoil_Michaud" title="Joseph-Enoil Michaud">Joseph-Enoil Michaud</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Succeeded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data">James Lorimer Ilsley</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1218072481"></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em;"><a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Canada)" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs (Canada)">Secretary of State for External Affairs</a></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b>In office</b></span><br />September 4, 1946&#160;–&#32;September 9, 1948</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1218072481"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Prime Minister</span></th><td class="infobox-data">W. L. Mackenzie King</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data">W. L. Pearson</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1218072481"></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em;"><a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">Member of Parliament</a><br />for <a href="/wiki/Quebec_East" title="Quebec East">Quebec East</a></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b>In office</b></span><br />February 9, 1942&#160;–&#32;March 31, 1958</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1218072481"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Ernest_Lapointe" title="Ernest Lapointe">Ernest Lapointe</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Succeeded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Yvon-Roma_Tass%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Yvon-Roma Tassé">Yvon-Roma Tassé</a></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Louis Stephen St-Laurent</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1882-02-01</span>)</span>February 1, 1882<br /><a href="/wiki/Compton,_Quebec" title="Compton, Quebec">Compton, Quebec</a>, Canada</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">July 25, 1973<span style="display:none">(1973-07-25)</span> (aged&#160;91)<br /><a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>, Quebec, Canada</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/St._Thomas_Aquinas_Cemetery" title="St. Thomas Aquinas Cemetery">Saint Thomas d'Aquin Cemetery</a>, Compton, Quebec</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;margin-top:1px;white-space:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Jeanne_Renault" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeanne Renault">Jeanne Renault</a></div>
   <div class="marriage-line-margin2px">&#8203;</div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;margin-bottom:1px;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;1908&#59;&#32;died&#160;1966&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Children</th><td class="infobox-data">5, including <a ol{counter-reset:listitem}.mw-parser-output .hlist ol>li{counter-increment:listitem}.mw-parser-output .hlist ol>li::before{content:" "counter(listitem)"\a0 "}.mw-parser-output .hlist dd class="hlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/S%C3%A9minaire_de_Sherbrooke" title="Séminaire de Sherbrooke">Séminaire Saint-Charles-Borromée</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Université Laval</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Profession</th><td class="infobox-data">Lawyer</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span class="skin-invert" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Louis St. Laurent&#39;s signature"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg/128px-Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="25" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg/192px-Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg/256px-Louis_St-Laurent_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="196" data-file-height="39" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right;"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Louis Stephen St. Laurent</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Counsel" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Counsel">QC</a></span> (<i>Saint-Laurent</i> or <i>St-Laurent</i> in French, baptized <b>Louis-Étienne St-Laurent</b>; February 1, 1882&#160;– July 25, 1973) was a Canadian lawyer and politician who served as the 12th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1948 to 1957.
</p><p>Born and raised in southeastern <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, St. Laurent was a leading lawyer and a supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party of Canada</a>. In December 1941, he entered politics as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada" title="Minister of Justice and Attorney General of Canada">minister of justice</a> under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a>. In February 1942, he won a by-election in the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_(Canada)" title="Electoral district (Canada)">riding</a> of <a href="/wiki/Quebec_East" title="Quebec East">Quebec East</a>. In September 1946, St. Laurent became <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs">secretary of state for external affairs</a> and served in that post until <a href="/wiki/1948_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1948 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">two years later</a>, when he became leader of the Liberal Party and prime minister, succeeding King who retired. St. Laurent carried the party to back-to-back <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide</a> <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority governments</a> in the federal elections of <a href="/wiki/1949_Canadian_federal_election" title="1949 Canadian federal election">1949</a> and <a href="/wiki/1953_Canadian_federal_election" title="1953 Canadian federal election">1953</a>.
</p><p>The second <a href="/wiki/French_Canadians" title="French Canadians">French Canadian</a> to hold the office, St. Laurent strongly advocated against <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a> and was an enthusiastic proponent of Canada joining <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a> in 1949 to fight the spread of the ideology. His government also contributed troops to the <a href="/wiki/Korean_War" title="Korean War">Korean War</a>. At home, St. Laurent's government introduced the <a href="/wiki/Registered_retirement_savings_plan" title="Registered retirement savings plan">registered retirement savings plan</a> (RRSP) and oversaw the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Highway" title="Trans-Canada Highway">Trans-Canada Highway</a>, <a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence_Seaway" title="St. Lawrence Seaway">St. Lawrence Seaway</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Pipeline" class="mw-redirect" title="Trans-Canada Pipeline">Trans-Canada Pipeline</a>. St. Laurent earned the nickname "Uncle Louis" as he was popular among the general public throughout his tenure, and the popularity of his government led many to predict that he would easily win the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 federal election</a>. However, his decision to rush the <a href="/wiki/Pipeline_Debate" title="Pipeline Debate">1956 debate on the Trans-Canada Pipeline</a> by invoking <a href="/wiki/Cloture" title="Cloture">closure</a> led some to believe that the Liberals had become arrogant from their two decades in power, and in a major <a href="/wiki/Upset_victory" class="mw-redirect" title="Upset victory">upset</a>, the party was narrowly defeated by <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a>, ending nearly 22 years of Liberal rule. Shortly after his defeat, St. Laurent retired from politics and returned to his law practice. He is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">ranked</a> highly among analysts, not least because of his progressive programs and fiscally responsible policies that helped shape <a href="/wiki/History_of_Canada_(1945%E2%80%931960)" title="History of Canada (1945–1960)">post-war Canada</a>. According to historian <a href="/wiki/Donald_Creighton" title="Donald Creighton">Donald Creighton</a>, he was an "eminently moderate, cautious...man...and a strong <a href="/wiki/Canadian_nationalism" title="Canadian nationalism">Canadian nationalist</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life.2C_family.2C_and_education_.281882.E2.80.931905.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life,_family,_and_education_(1882–1905)">Early life, family, and education (1882–1905)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life, family, and education (1882–1905)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg/172px-Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg" decoding="async" width="172" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg/258px-Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg/344px-Jeanne_Renault_and_Louis_St._Laurent.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="511" /></a><figcaption>Louis and Jeanne on their wedding day, May 19, 1908</figcaption></figure>
<p>Louis St. Laurent (<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small">French pronunciation:</span> <span class="IPA nowrap" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">&#91;lwi<span class="wrap"> </span>sɛ̃<span class="wrap"> </span>lɔʁɑ̃&#93;</a></span>) was born on February 1, 1882, in <a href="/wiki/Compton,_Quebec" title="Compton, Quebec">Compton, Quebec</a>, a village in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Townships" title="Eastern Townships">Eastern Townships</a>, to Jean-Baptiste-Moïse Saint-Laurent, a <a href="/wiki/French_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="French Canadian">French Canadian</a>, and Mary Anne Broderick, an <a href="/wiki/Irish_Canadians" title="Irish Canadians">Irish Canadian</a>. Louis was the oldest of seven children. At the time of his birth, Compton was mainly English-speaking, though it would slowly become majority French between 1901 and 1911. St. Laurent grew up fluently <a href="/wiki/Bilingual" class="mw-redirect" title="Bilingual">bilingual</a>, as his father spoke French while his mother only spoke English. His English had a noticeable Irish <a href="/wiki/Brogue_(accent)" title="Brogue (accent)">brogue</a>, while his gestures (such as a hunch of the shoulders) were French.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutchison1964288_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchison1964288-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent was also interested in English literature as a child. The St. Laurent home would serve as a social centre for the village.<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Laurent's father, Jean-Baptiste, was a Compton shopkeeper and a staunch supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party of Canada</a> and Sir <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>. Jean-Baptiste would unsuccessfully run in a provincial by-election in 1894.<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> When Laurier led the Liberals to victory in the <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896 election</a>, 14-year-old Louis relayed the election returns from the telephone in his father's store.
</p><p>St. Laurent received degrees from <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A9minaire_de_Sherbrooke" title="Séminaire de Sherbrooke">Séminaire Saint-Charles-Borromée</a><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> (B.A. 1902) and <span title="French-language text"><span lang="fr" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Université Laval</a></span></span> (LL.L. 1905). He was offered, but declined, a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship" title="Rhodes Scholarship">Rhodes Scholarship</a> upon this graduation from Laval in 1905. In 1908, he married <a href="/wiki/Jeanne_St._Laurent" title="Jeanne St. Laurent">Jeanne Renault</a> (1886–1966), with whom he had two sons and three daughters, including <a href="/wiki/Jean-Paul_St._Laurent" title="Jean-Paul St. Laurent">Jean-Paul St. Laurent</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECookBélanger2007_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECookBélanger2007-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Legal_career_.281905.E2.80.931942.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Legal_career_(1905–1942)">Legal career (1905–1942)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Legal career (1905–1942)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cropped_Louis.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/98/Cropped_Louis.jpg" decoding="async" width="101" height="139" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="101" data-file-height="139" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent as a lawyer</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent worked as a lawyer from 1905 to 1942. He also became a professor of law at Université Laval in 1914. St. Laurent practised <a href="/wiki/Corporate_law" title="Corporate law">corporate</a>, <a href="/wiki/Commercial_law" title="Commercial law">commercial</a> and <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_law" title="Constitutional law">constitutional law</a> in Quebec and became one of the country's most respected counsel. St. Laurent served as president of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Bar_Association" title="Canadian Bar Association">Canadian Bar Association</a> from 1930 to 1932.<sup id="cite_ref-cba_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cba-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1907, St. Laurent gained some attention in Quebec after he made a move that was viewed unusual at the time: he put a priest and nuns on the witness stand and <a href="/wiki/Cross-examination" title="Cross-examination">cross-examined</a> them. This occurred during his engagement in a case contesting the will of a woman who had left everything she owned to her parish priest.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> In 1912, St. Laurent won a case against <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Railway" title="Canadian Pacific Railway">Canadian Pacific</a>. In 1913, he was one of the defending counsel for <a href="/wiki/Harry_Kendall_Thaw" title="Harry Kendall Thaw">Harry Kendall Thaw</a>, who was seeking to avoid extradition from Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-Thaw_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thaw-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
In 1923, St. Laurent opened his own law office.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In 1926, in a test case before the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court</a>, St. Laurent argued for religious minority (non-<a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Canada" title="Christianity in Canada">Christian</a>) rights. He was in favour of <a href="/wiki/Judaism_in_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Judaism in Canada">Jewish</a> demand for representation on Montreal’s Protestant Board of School Commissioners and he also supported a separate Jewish system of schools. Though St. Laurent's bid to have Jewish representation in the school board was unsuccessful, the province of Quebec recognized the right to establish separate schools for non-Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though an ardent Liberal, Louis remained aloof from active politics for much of his life, focusing instead on his legal career and family. He became one of Quebec's leading lawyers and was so highly regarded that he was twice offered a seat as a justice on the Supreme Court of Canada, offers he declined.
</p>
<h2><span id="Cabinet_minister_.281942.E2.80.931948.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_minister_(1942–1948)">Cabinet minister (1942–1948)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Cabinet minister (1942–1948)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Justice">Minister of Justice</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Minister of Justice"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
It was not until he was nearly 60 that St. Laurent finally agreed to enter politics when Liberal <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a> appealed to his sense of duty in late 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> King's <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Lapointe" title="Ernest Lapointe">Ernest Lapointe</a>, had died in November 1941. King believed that his Quebec lieutenant had to be strong enough and respected enough to help deal with the volatile conscription issue. King had been a junior politician when he witnessed the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a> during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> and wanted to prevent the same divisions from threatening his government. Many recommended St. Laurent for the post. On these recommendations, King recruited St. Laurent to cabinet as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">Minister of Justice</a>, Lapointe's former post, on 9 December. St. Laurent agreed to go to Ottawa out of a sense of duty, but only on the understanding that his foray into politics was temporary and that he would return to Quebec at the conclusion of the war. In February 1942, he won a by-election for <a href="/wiki/Quebec_East" title="Quebec East">Quebec East</a>, Lapointe's former riding, which had been previously held by Laurier. St. Laurent supported King's decision to introduce <a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a> in 1944 (see <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1944" title="Conscription Crisis of 1944">Conscription Crisis of 1944</a>). His support prevented more than a handful of Quebec Liberal <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">Members of Parliament</a> (MPs) from leaving the party and was therefore crucial to keeping the government and the party united.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECookBélanger2007251–355_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECookBélanger2007251–355-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent was King's right-hand man.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StLaurentKing1945.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/StLaurentKing1945.jpg/200px-StLaurentKing1945.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/StLaurentKing1945.jpg/300px-StLaurentKing1945.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/StLaurentKing1945.jpg/400px-StLaurentKing1945.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent (left) and Prime Minister King (right) at the San Francisco Conference, May 1945</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent represented Canada at the <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="San Francisco Conference">1945 San Francisco Conference</a> that helped lead to the founding of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> (UN).<sup id="cite_ref-The_United_Nations_Organization_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_United_Nations_Organization-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1944, St. Laurent oversaw the creation of family allowances. In 1945, St. Laurent supported a program of economic reconstruction and more social welfare, which consisted of federal-provincial cost-sharing schemes for old-age pensions and hospital and medical insurance. Some officials were worried that these sweeping changes would cause disputes between the federal and provincial governments, but St. Laurent believed that Canadians identified with and supported these programs, stating that "[they] were constantly made aware of the services which provincial governments render while they tended to think of the central government as one imposing burdens such as taxation and conscription."<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1945, Soviet cipher clerk <a href="/wiki/Igor_Gouzenko" title="Igor Gouzenko">Igor Gouzenko</a> unexpectedly arrived at St. Laurent's office with evidence of a Soviet spy ring operating in Canada, the United States, and the United Kingdom. Known as the <a href="/wiki/Gouzenko_Affair" title="Gouzenko Affair">Gouzenko Affair</a>, the revelations and subsequent investigations over the following few years showed major Soviet <a href="/wiki/Espionage" title="Espionage">espionage</a> in North America.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_external_affairs">Minister of external affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Minister of external affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>King came to regard St. Laurent as his most trusted minister and natural successor. He persuaded St. Laurent that it was his duty to remain in government following the war in order to help with the construction of a post-war international order and promoted him to the position of <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for External Affairs">secretary of state for external affairs</a> (foreign minister) in 1946, a portfolio King had always kept for himself.
</p><p>In January 1947, St. Laurent delivered a speech at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a>, highlighting the need for an independent Canadian foreign policy that would not always rely on the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a>. St. Laurent's speech implied that Canadian foreign policy was only an extension of British foreign policy. He also said that Canada should have the “willingness to accept international responsibilities.”<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="United_Nations">United Nations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: United Nations"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg/225px-NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg" decoding="async" width="225" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg/338px-NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg/450px-NewfoundlandNegotiators1947.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="499" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent (bottom, centre-right) and King (bottom, centre) and other delegates negotiating the entry of Newfoundland and Labrador into Confederation, 1947</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent, compelled by his belief that the UN would be ineffective in times of war and armed conflict without some military means to impose its will, advocated the adoption of a UN military force. This force he proposed would be used in situations that called for both tact and might to preserve peace or prevent combat. In 1956, this idea was actualized by St. Laurent and his secretary of state for external affairs, <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson" title="Lester B. Pearson">Lester B. Pearson</a>, in the development of UN peacekeepers that helped to put an end to the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-The_United_Nations_Organization_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_United_Nations_Organization-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Laurent also believed that the UN was failing to provide international security from <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>. He therefore proposed an Atlantic security organization that would supplement the UN. That would become reality in 1949, when the <a href="/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization" class="mw-redirect" title="North Atlantic Treaty Organization">North Atlantic Treaty Organization</a> (NATO) was founded. St. Laurent is seen as one of the first people in power to propose such an institution.<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Annexation_of_Newfoundland">Annexation of Newfoundland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Annexation of Newfoundland"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>St. Laurent was a strong supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_Newfoundland" title="Dominion of Newfoundland">Dominion of Newfoundland</a> joining Canada. He ignored objections from the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Quebec" title="Government of Quebec">government of Quebec</a>, which had land claims against Newfoundland and demanded a right of veto over the admission of any new province or territory. St. Laurent led two negotiations with Newfoundland and <a href="/wiki/Joey_Smallwood" title="Joey Smallwood">Joey Smallwood</a> in the summer of 1947 and the fall of 1948. These negotiations were successful, and on March 31, 1949, Canada annexed Newfoundland and Labrador, with St. Laurent presiding over the ceremonies in Ottawa as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1948_Liberal_Party_leadership_convention">1948 Liberal Party leadership convention</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1948 Liberal Party leadership convention"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg/190px-Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg/285px-Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg/380px-Louis_Saint-Laurent_7_aout_1948.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="497" /></a><figcaption>Louis St. Laurent, August 7, 1948</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1948, MacKenzie King retired after over 21 years in power, and quietly persuaded his senior ministers to support St. Laurent's selection as the new Liberal leader at the <a href="/wiki/1948_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1948 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">Liberal leadership convention</a> that took place on August 7, 1948, exactly 29 years after King <a href="/wiki/1919_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1919 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">became leader</a>. St. Laurent easily won, defeating two other opponents.
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281948.E2.80.931957.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1948–1957)">Prime Minister (1948–1957)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1948–1957)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>St. Laurent was sworn in as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> on 15 November 1948, making him Canada's second French Canadian prime minister, after <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>.
</p><p>St. Laurent was the first prime minister to live in the official residence of the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister of Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/24_Sussex_Drive" title="24 Sussex Drive">24 Sussex Drive</a> (then known as 24 Sussex Street), from 1951 to 1957.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Federal_election_victories">Federal election victories</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Federal election victories"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1949_federal_election">1949 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 1949 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg/220px-Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="274" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg/330px-Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a1/Louis_Saint-Laurent_1949.jpg 2x" data-file-width="385" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent shaking hands with supporters during the 1949 election campaign</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent's first mission was to give the Liberals a new mandate. In the <a href="/wiki/1949_Canadian_federal_election" title="1949 Canadian federal election">1949 federal election</a> that followed his ascension to the Liberal leadership, many wondered, including Liberal Party insiders, if St. Laurent would appeal to the post-war populace of Canada. On the campaign trail, St. Laurent's image was developed into somewhat of a 'character' and what is considered to be the first 'media image' to be used in Canadian politics. St. Laurent chatted with children, gave speeches in his shirt sleeves, and had a 'common touch' that turned out to be appealing to voters. At one event during the 1949 election campaign, he disembarked his train and instead of approaching the assembled crowd of adults and reporters, gravitated to, and began chatting with, a group of children on the platform. A reporter submitted an article entitled "Uncle Louis can't lose!" which earned him the nickname "Uncle Louis" in the media ("Papa Louis" in Quebec).<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> With this common touch and broad appeal, he led the party to victory in the election against the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative Party</a> (PC Party) led by <a href="/wiki/George_A._Drew" title="George A. Drew">George Drew</a>. The Liberals won 191 seats – the most in Canadian history at the time, and still a record for the party. This is also the Liberals' second-most successful result in their history in terms of proportion of seats, behind the <a href="/wiki/1940_Canadian_federal_election" title="1940 Canadian federal election">1940 federal election</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1953_federal_election">1953 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 1953 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>St. Laurent led the Liberals to another powerful majority in the <a href="/wiki/1953_Canadian_federal_election" title="1953 Canadian federal election">1953 federal election</a>, once again defeating PC leader Drew. Though they lost 22 seats, they still had three dozen seats more than the number needed for a majority, enabling them to dominate the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Foreign policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/220px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/330px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/440px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg 2x" data-file-width="760" data-file-height="597" /></a><figcaption>Canadian Prime Minister St. Laurent (far left), British prime minister <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> (left), British foreign minister <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> (right), and Canadian foreign minister <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a> (far right) in Ottawa in 1954</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent and his cabinet oversaw Canada's expanding international role in the postwar world. His stated desire was for Canada to occupy a social, military, and economic <a href="/wiki/Middle_power" title="Middle power">middle power</a> role in the post-World War II world. In 1947, he identified the five basic principles of Canadian foreign policy and five practical applications regarding Canada's international relations. Always highly sensitive to cleavages of language, religion, and region, he stressed national unity, insisting, "that our external policies shall not destroy our unity&#160;... for a disunited Canada will be a powerless one."  He also stressed political liberty and rule of law in the sense of opposition to totalitarianism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMackenzie2007_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackenzie2007-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Militarily, St. Laurent was a leading proponent of the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization" class="mw-redirect" title="North Atlantic Treaty Organization">North Atlantic Treaty Organization</a> (NATO) in 1949, serving as an architect and signatory of the treaty document.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Involvement in such an organization marked a departure from King who had been reticent about joining a military alliance. Under his leadership, Canada supported the United Nations (UN) in the <a href="/wiki/Korean_War" title="Korean War">Korean War</a> and committed the third largest overall contribution of troops, ships and aircraft to the U.N. forces to the conflict. Troops to Korea were selected on a voluntary basis. St. Laurent sent over 26,000 troops to fight in the war. In 1956, under his direction, St. Laurent's secretary of state for external affairs, Lester B. Pearson, helped solve the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a> between Great Britain, France, <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, bringing forward St. Laurent's 1946 views on a U.N. military force in the form of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Emergency_Force" title="United Nations Emergency Force">United Nations Emergency Force</a> (UNEF) or <a href="/wiki/Peacekeeping" title="Peacekeeping">peacekeeping</a>. These actions were recognized when Pearson won the 1957 <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-The_United_Nations_Organization_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_United_Nations_Organization-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early 1954, St. Laurent took a 42-day long tour around the world, citing his desire to get a better picture of what he said, "the problems which all of us have to face together." He visited 12 countries in total, including France, Germany, Japan, India, and Pakistan. When he returned to Canada, St. Laurent's personality and character appeared to slightly change; cabinet ministers noticed he showed signs of fatigue and indifference. Some even claimed he started to feel depressed. Author <a href="/wiki/Dale_C._Thomson" title="Dale C. Thomson">Dale C. Thomson</a> wrote, "[the tour was] his greatest hour but it marked as well the beginning of his decline; as such, it was a turning point both for him and for Canadian politics."<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Economic policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louisstlaurent.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Louisstlaurent.jpg/175px-Louisstlaurent.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="219" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Louisstlaurent.jpg/263px-Louisstlaurent.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Louisstlaurent.jpg/350px-Louisstlaurent.jpg 2x" data-file-width="383" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent, 1950</figcaption></figure>
<p>It took taxation surpluses no longer needed by the wartime military and paying back in full Canada's debts accrued during the World Wars and the Great Depression. With remaining revenues, St. Laurent oversaw the expansion of Canada's social programs, including the gradual expansion of social welfare programs such as family allowances, old age pensions, government funding of university and post-secondary education and an early form of <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Canada)" title="Medicare (Canada)">Medicare</a> termed <i>Hospital Insurance</i> at the time. This scheme laid the groundwork for <a href="/wiki/Tommy_Douglas" title="Tommy Douglas">Tommy Douglas</a>' healthcare system in Saskatchewan, and Pearson's nationwide universal healthcare in the late 1960s. Under this legislation, the federal government paid around 50% of the cost of provincial health plans to cover "a basic range of inpatient services in acute, convalescent, and chronic hospital care." The condition for the cost-sharing agreements was that all citizens were to be entitled to these benefits, and by March 1963, 98.8% of Canadians were covered by <i>Hospital Insurance</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Guest_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guest-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> According to historian Katherine Boothe, however, St. Laurent did not regard government health insurance to be a "good policy idea", instead favouring the expansion of voluntary insurance through existing plans. In 1951, for instance, St. Laurent spoke in support of the medical profession assuming "the administration and responsibility for, a scheme that would provide prepaid medical attendance to any Canadian who needed it".<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition, St. Laurent modernized and established new social and industrial policies for the country during his time in the prime minister's office. Amongst these measures included the universalization of old-age pensions for all Canadians aged seventy and above (1951),<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> the introduction of old age assistance for needy Canadians aged sixty-five and above (1951),<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> the introduction of allowances for the blind (1951) and the disabled (1954),<sup id="cite_ref-Guest_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guest-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> amendments to the National Housing Act (1954) which provided federal government financing to non-profit organisations as well as the provinces for the renovation or construction of hostels or housing for students, the disabled, the elderly, and families on low incomes,<sup id="cite_ref-Guest_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guest-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and unemployment assistance (1956) for unemployed employables on welfare who had exhausted (or did not qualify for) unemployment insurance benefits.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> During his last term as prime minister, St. Laurent's government used $100&#160;million in death taxes to establish the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Council" title="Canada Council">Canada Council</a> to support research in the arts, humanities, and social sciences. In 1956, using the taxation authority of the federal level of government, St. Laurent's government introduced the policy of "<a href="/wiki/Equalization_payments_in_Canada" title="Equalization payments in Canada">equalization payments</a>" which redistributes taxation revenues between provinces to assist the poorer provinces in delivering government programs and services, a move that has been considered a strong one in solidifying the Canadian federation, particularly with his home province of <a href="/wiki/Qu%C3%A9bec" class="mw-redirect" title="Québec">Québec</a>.
</p><p>In 1957, St. Laurent's government introduced the <a href="/wiki/Registered_retirement_savings_plan" title="Registered retirement savings plan">registered retirement savings plan (RRSP)</a>, a type of financial account used to hold savings and investment assets. The plan had many tax advantages and was designed to promote savings for retirement by employees and self-employed people.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Immigration"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1948, St. Laurent's government dramatically increased <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada" title="Immigration to Canada">immigration</a> in order to expand Canada's labour base. St. Laurent believed that immigration was key to post-war economic growth. He also believed that immigration would create a sufficient tax base that would pay for social welfare measures that were established at the end of World War II. Over 125,000 immigrants arrived in Canada in 1948 alone, and that number would more than double to 282,000 in 1957. Large numbers of immigrants were from Southern Europe, including <a href="/wiki/Italians" title="Italians">Italians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greeks</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_people" title="Portuguese people">Portuguese</a> immigrants. Their arrival shifted the balance of ethnic origins amongst Canadians, increasing the population who were of neither French nor British descent.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1956 and 1957, Canada received over 37,500 refugees from <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a>, in the wake of the <a href="/wiki/1956_Hungarian_Revolution" class="mw-redirect" title="1956 Hungarian Revolution">1956 Hungarian Revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Infrastructure">Infrastructure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Infrastructure"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_(cropped).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png/220px-St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png/330px-St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png/440px-St._Lawrence_Seaway_construction_1959_%28cropped%29.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="807" /></a><figcaption>The construction of the St. Lawrence Seaway in 1959</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent's government engaged in massive public works and infrastructure projects such as building the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Highway" title="Trans-Canada Highway">Trans-Canada Highway</a> (1949), the <a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence_Seaway" title="St. Lawrence Seaway">St. Lawrence Seaway</a> (1954) and the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Pipeline" class="mw-redirect" title="Trans-Canada Pipeline">Trans-Canada Pipeline</a>. It was this last project that was to sow the seeds that led to the downfall of the St. Laurent government.
</p><p>St. Laurent had to go through a series of negotiations with the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> in order to start the construction of the St. Lawrence Seaway. In order to negotiate with the U.S., St. Laurent met with president <a href="/wiki/Harry_S._Truman" title="Harry S. Truman">Harry S. Truman</a> twice, in 1949 and 1951, but was unsuccessful both times. St. Laurent then threatened that Canada would build the seaway alone. Finally, in 1953 and 1954, Truman's successor, president <a href="/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower" class="mw-redirect" title="Dwight Eisenhower">Dwight Eisenhower</a>, secured a deal with St. Laurent. The deal costed $470 million <a href="/wiki/Canadian_dollar" title="Canadian dollar">Canadian dollars</a>, with Canada paying nearly three-fourths of that total and the U.S. paying about one-fourth. The seaway was completed in 1959 and expanded Canada's economic trade routes with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_domestic_affairs">Other domestic affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Other domestic affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1949, the former lawyer of many Supreme Court cases, St. Laurent ended the practice of appealing Canadian legal cases to the <a href="/wiki/Judicial_Committee_of_the_Privy_Council" title="Judicial Committee of the Privy Council">Judicial Committee of the Privy Council</a> of Great Britain, making the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> the highest avenue of legal appeal available to Canadians. In that same year, St. Laurent negotiated the <a href="/wiki/British_North_America_Acts" title="British North America Acts">British North America (No. 2) Act, 1949</a> with Britain which 'partially patriated' the Canadian Constitution, most significantly giving the Canadian Parliament the authority to amend portions of the constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1949, following <a href="/wiki/1948_Newfoundland_referendums" title="1948 Newfoundland referendums">two referendums</a> within the province, St. Laurent and Premier <a href="/wiki/Joey_Smallwood" title="Joey Smallwood">Joey Smallwood</a> negotiated the entry of Newfoundland and Labrador into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>.
</p><p>When asked in 1949 whether he would outlaw the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Communist Party (Canada)">Communist Party</a> in Canada, St. Laurent responded that the party posed little threat and that such measures would be drastic.<sup id="cite_ref-google_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-google-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1952, St. Laurent advised <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth II of the United Kingdom">Queen Elizabeth II</a> to appoint <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a> as the first Canadian-born <a href="/wiki/Governor-General" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor-General">Governor-General</a>. Each of the aforementioned actions were and are seen as significant in furthering the cause of Canadian autonomy from Britain and developing a national identity on the international stage.
</p><p>In 1953, St. Laurent undertook the <a href="/wiki/High_Arctic_relocation" title="High Arctic relocation">High Arctic relocation</a>, where 92 <a href="/wiki/Inuit" title="Inuit">Inuit</a> were moved from <a href="/wiki/Inukjuak" title="Inukjuak">Inukjuak, Quebec</a> to two communities in the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">Northwest Territories</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Nunavut" title="Nunavut">Nunavut</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-report_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-report-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-porteous_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-porteous-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The relocation was a <a href="/wiki/Forced_displacement" title="Forced displacement">forced migration</a> instigated by the federal government to assert its sovereignty in the Far North by the use of "human flagpoles",<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> in light of both the Cold War and the <a href="/wiki/Territorial_claims_in_the_Arctic" title="Territorial claims in the Arctic">disputed territorial</a> claims to the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Arctic_Archipelago" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Arctic Archipelago">Canadian Arctic Archipelago</a>. The relocated Inuit were not given sufficient support to prevent extreme <a href="/wiki/Privation" title="Privation">privation</a> during their first years after the move. The story was the subject of a book called <i>The Long Exile</i>, published by <a href="/wiki/Melanie_McGrath" title="Melanie McGrath">Melanie McGrath</a> in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-McGrath2006_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McGrath2006-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Defeat_in_the_1957_federal_election">Defeat in the 1957 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Defeat in the 1957 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Pipeline_Debate">Pipeline Debate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Pipeline Debate"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Pipeline_Debate" title="Pipeline Debate">Pipeline Debate</a></div>
<p>The 1956 <a href="/wiki/Pipeline_Debate" title="Pipeline Debate">Pipeline Debate</a> led to the widespread impression that the Liberals had grown arrogant in power. On numerous occasions, the government invoked <a href="/wiki/Cloture" title="Cloture">closure</a> in order to curtail debate and ensure that its Pipeline Bill passed by a specific deadline. St. Laurent was criticized for a lack of restraint exercised on his minister, <a href="/wiki/C._D._Howe" title="C. D. Howe">C.&#160;D. Howe</a> (who was also known as the "Minister of Everything"). Howe was widely perceived as extremely arrogant. Western Canadians felt particularly alienated by the government, believing that the Liberals were kowtowing to interests in Ontario and Quebec and the United States. The opposition accused the government of accepting overly costly contracts that could never be completed on schedule. In the end, the pipeline was completed early and under budget. The pipeline conflict turned out to be meaningless, insofar as the construction work was concerned, since pipe could not be obtained in 1956 from a striking American factory, and no work could have been done that year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutchison1964303–307_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchison1964303–307-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The uproar in Parliament regarding the pipeline had a lasting impression on the electorate, and was a decisive factor in the Liberal government's 1957 defeat at the hands of the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative (PC) Party</a>, led by <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 election</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Results">Results</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Results"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_and_John_-_1957_(cropped).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png/220px-Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png/330px-Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png/440px-Louis_and_John_-_1957_%28cropped%29.png 2x" data-file-width="1388" data-file-height="1126" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent shaking hands with Diefenbaker on June 14, 1957 in Ottawa, just days after the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">federal election</a> in which Diefenbaker defeated St. Laurent in a stunning upset</figcaption></figure>
<p>By 1957 St. Laurent was 75 years old and tired. His party had been in power for 22 years, and by this time had accumulated too many factions and alienated too many groups. He was ready to retire, but was persuaded to fight one last campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 election</a>, the Liberals won 200,000 more votes nationwide than the Progressive Conservatives (40.75% Liberals to 38.81% PC). However, a large portion of that overall Liberal popular vote came from huge majorities in Quebec ridings, and did not translate into seats in other parts of the country. Largely due to dominating the rest of the country, the Progressive Conservatives took the greatest number of seats with 112 seats (42% of the House) to the Liberals' 105 (39.2%). The result of the election came as a shock to many, and is considered to be one of the greatest <a href="/wiki/Upset_victory" class="mw-redirect" title="Upset victory">upsets</a> in Canadian federal political history.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some ministers wanted St. Laurent to stay on and offer to form a minority government, arguing that the popular vote had supported them and the party's long years of experience would make them a more effective minority. Another option circulated within the party saw the balance of power to be held by either the <a href="/wiki/Co-operative_Commonwealth_Federation" title="Co-operative Commonwealth Federation">Co-operative Commonwealth Federation</a> (CCF) and their 25 seats or <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit_Party_of_Canada" title="Social Credit Party of Canada">Social Credit Party of Canada</a> with their 15 seats. St. Laurent was encouraged by others to reach out to the CCF and at least four of six independent/small party MPs to form a coalition majority government, which would have held 134 of the 265 seats in Parliament—50.6% of the total. St. Laurent, however, had no desire to stay in office; he believed that the nation had passed a verdict against his government and his party. In any case, the CCF and Socreds had pledged to cooperate with a Tory government. It was very likely that St. Laurent would have been defeated on the floor of the House had he tried to stay in power with a minority government, and would not have stayed in office for long even if he survived that confidence vote. With this in mind, St. Laurent resigned on 21 June 1957—ending the longest uninterrupted run in government for a party at the federal level in Canadian history.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg/170px-Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="277" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg/255px-Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg/340px-Louis_St._Laurent_statue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2108" data-file-height="3429" /></a><figcaption>Statue on grounds of the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent chose the following jurists to be appointed as justices of the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a>:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/John_Robert_Cartwright" title="John Robert Cartwright">John Robert Cartwright</a> (22 December 1949 – 23 March 1970)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Joseph_Honor%C3%A9_G%C3%A9rald_Fauteux" class="mw-redirect" title="Joseph Honoré Gérald Fauteux">Joseph Honoré Gérald Fauteux</a> (22 December 1949 – 23 December 1973)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Douglas_Charles_Abbott" class="mw-redirect" title="Douglas Charles Abbott">Douglas Charles Abbott</a> (1 July 1954 – 23 December 1973)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Patrick_Kerwin" title="Patrick Kerwin">Patrick Kerwin</a> (as Chief Justice, 1 July 1954 – 2 February 1963; appointed a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_Justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne Justice">Puisne Justice</a> under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/R._B._Bennett" title="R. B. Bennett">Richard Bennett</a>, 20 July 1935)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Henry_Grattan_Nolan" title="Henry Grattan Nolan">Henry Grattan Nolan</a> (1 March 1956 – 8 July 1957)</li></ul>
<h2><span id="Retirement_and_death_.281957.E2.80.931973.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement_and_death_(1957–1973)">Retirement and death (1957–1973)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Retirement and death (1957–1973)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Laurent_house.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/St_Laurent_house.jpg/220px-St_Laurent_house.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/St_Laurent_house.jpg/330px-St_Laurent_house.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/St_Laurent_house.jpg/440px-St_Laurent_house.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>201 Grande-Allée, residence of St. Laurent in Quebec City for sixty years</figcaption></figure>
<p>After a short period as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Leader of the Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Opposition</a> and now more than 75 years old, St. Laurent's motivation to be involved in politics was gone. He announced his intention to retire from politics. He was succeeded as Liberal Party leader by his former secretary of state for external affairs and representative at the United Nations, <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson" title="Lester B. Pearson">Lester B. Pearson</a>, at the party's <a href="/wiki/1958_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1958 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership convention</a> in January 1958.
</p><p>St. Laurent preferred law over politics. In a 1961 interview with the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">CBC</a>, he stated, "One can be more outspoken, frank and sincere before the courts than he could be before the public audience in a political campaign."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In that same interview, St. Laurent acknowledged that the Pipeline Debate played a major role in his 1957 loss, stating, "Perhaps I didn't say as much as I should have; people do make mistakes you know. I did my best and, as a matter of fact, we had become accustomed to carry on as a board of directors and that displeased a part of the Canadian public." St. Laurent admitted that it took a while to resume his good mood after a sudden electoral loss.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After his political retirement, he returned to practising law and living quietly and privately with his family. During his retirement, he was called into the public spotlight one final time in 1967 to be made a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a>, a newly created award.
</p><p>St. Laurent was appointed a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> on July 6, 1967. His citation reads:<sup id="cite_ref-archive_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archive-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>Former Prime Minister of Canada. For his service to his country.</p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Large rectangular grey granite stone inscribed with names, birth years, and death years, of St. Laurent and his wife" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg/220px-Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="116" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg/330px-Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg/440px-Louis_St._Laurent_grave_site.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2346" data-file-height="1239" /></a><figcaption>St. Laurent's grave site</figcaption></figure>
<p>Louis Stephen St. Laurent died from heart failure on July 25, 1973, in <a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, aged 91 and was buried at <a href="/wiki/St._Thomas_Aquinas_Cemetery" title="St. Thomas Aquinas Cemetery">Saint Thomas d'Aquin Cemetery</a> in his hometown of <a href="/wiki/Compton,_Quebec" title="Compton, Quebec">Compton, Quebec</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Parks_Grave_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parks_Grave-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_memorials">Legacy and memorials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_St._Laurent&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Legacy and memorials"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>St. Laurent presided over the beginning of a new period in Canadian history, <a href="/wiki/History_of_Canada_(1945%E2%80%931960)" title="History of Canada (1945–1960)">post-WW2 Canada</a>. Many have referred to this period as "Canada's Golden Age".<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent's government was modestly progressive, fiscally responsible, and run with business-like efficiency. St. Laurent's former senior servant, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gordon_Robertson" title="Robert Gordon Robertson">Robert Gordon Robertson</a>, wrote, "St Laurent's administrations from 1949 to 1956 probably gave Canada the most consistently good, financially responsible, trouble-free government the country has had in its entire history."<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> One of St. Laurent's cabinet ministers, <a href="/wiki/Jack_Pickersgill" title="Jack Pickersgill">Jack Pickersgill</a>, noted of him, "St. Laurent had made governing Canada look so easy that the people thought anyone could do it—and thus they elected John Diefenbaker."<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canadian author and professor, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bothwell" title="Robert Bothwell">Robert Bothwell</a>, wrote, "St. Laurent had many of the best characteristics of a prime minister but few of the best attributes of a politician. In his most productive years in the job, 1948 to 1954, he presided over a cabinet of strong ministers, many of them first-class politicians. His views and theirs generally coincided, though when they did not, it was the prime minister who prevailed. His fundamental commitment was to national unity, which he interpreted broadly in terms of an expansive federal power. At home and abroad he was an activist, which an abundant economy allowed him to be."<sup id="cite_ref-LouisBio_3-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LouisBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent,_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg/220px-CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="117" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg/330px-CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg/440px-CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent%2C_Heavy_Icebreaker.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3593" data-file-height="1905" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent" title="CCGS Louis S. St-Laurent">CCGS Louis S. St-Laurent</a>, heavy icebreaker</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent was ranked #4 on a survey of the first 20 prime ministers (through <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>) of Canada done by Canadian historians, and used by <a href="/wiki/J._L._Granatstein" title="J. L. Granatstein">J. L. Granatstein</a> and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a> in their book <i>Prime Ministers: Ranking Canada's Leaders</i>.
</p><p>The house and grounds in Compton where St. Laurent was born were designated a <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Sites_of_Canada" title="National Historic Sites of Canada">National Historic Site of Canada</a> in 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent's residence at 201 Grande-Allée Est in Quebec City is protected as a Recognized Federal Heritage Building.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/CCGS_Louis_S._St-Laurent" title="CCGS Louis S. St-Laurent">CCGS Louis S. St-Laurent</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Coast_Guard" title="Canadian Coast Guard">Canadian Coast Guard</a> <a href="/wiki/Icebreaker" title="Icebreaker">Heavy Arctic Icebreaker</a>, is named after him.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent_School" title="Louis St. Laurent School">Louis St. Laurent School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Edmonton" title="Edmonton">Edmonton</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a>. is named in his honour,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> as well as the Louis St-Laurent high school in <a href="/wiki/East_Angus,_Quebec" title="East Angus, Quebec">East Angus</a>, Quebec.
</p><p>The riding, <a href="/wiki/Louis-Saint-Laurent_(electoral_district)" title="Louis-Saint-Laurent (electoral district)">Louis-Saint-Laurent</a>, is named in his honour. Created in 2003, it partially consists of St. Laurent's old riding of Quebec East.
</p>
